INTRODUCTION TO THE BOYS AND GIRLS WHO WILL USE THIS BOOK 

This arithmetic book has been written for you. It presents the activities of your life and of the life of those about you, which cannot be understood without the use of arithmetic. Arithmetic is used in almost everything you do both in and out of school. 

Whenever you purchase groceries, you use arithmetic. Whenever you measure your weight, your height, or the foods that you eat, you use arithmetic. Whenever you use the calendar, the clock, the thermometer, or read the speedometer on your father's automobile, you use arithmetic. The farmer, the grocer, the banker, in fact everyone, uses arithmetic. 

First of all you must be able to perform the simple operations of arithmetic quickly and accurately. A great deal of practice and drill are necessary to give you skill in addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. You will find throughout this book many exercises to provide this practice. You will need to know what errors you make and why you make them. Tests are given which help you to locate your difficulties. Practice tests provide drill to be used to overcome these difficulties. 

The authors believe that boys and girls should solve real problems-problems which arise in your home life, in your school work, in your play and games, problems which your father and mother must meet and solve, and problems about trade, business, and government. The problems in this book are about things in which boys and girls are interested. In many of the groups of problems there are topics which you will wish to discuss with your parents and friends, or concerning which you will need to consult encyclopedias, reference books, and magazines. (iii)

iv 

INTRODUCTION TO THE TEACHER 

This book is for grade 5. The work of the fifth grade begins with a careful review of the work of grade 4. Diagnostic tests and remedial exercises make it possible to adjust the review to the needs of the individual pupils. Pupils are made conscious of their difficulties by purposeful and well motivated practice material. The standards aid the pupil to measure his progress. 

At the end of the grade there is a careful check up of the work that has been developed by standardized diagnostic tests by means of which the teacher can discover possible deficiencies in the work. 

THE DEVELOPMENT OF THE PROCESSES 

a. Special stress is placed on the careful grading of the steps in each of the processes of fractions and decimals so that the pupils encounter only one new difficulty at a time. b. Specific drill exercises are provided on the points which have been found by investigations to present special difficulty to pupils. c. Stress is placed on the use of illustrative examples to which the pupils may refer when in difficulty. d. Drill materials to fix each step in the development are provided, special stress being given to the elimination of the causes of error. e. After a step has been developed, special provision has been made to insure the retention of the skill by scientifically distributed drill in subsequent exercises. Carefully standardized practice tests at the ends of chapters make it possible both to measure the achievements of the pupils and to review the work that has been done. The problem material especially provides for the continuous use of the processes that have been learned. f. Provision is made for specific diagnostic exercises at the end of each development by means of which the pupils and teachers can make a careful check on the work that has been done. g. Objective tests for reviewing principles will be found at the end of the explanation of important development work.

INTRODUCTION 

h. In the development of each step the pupil is given specific directions as to procedure and a minimum of rationalization is undertaken. i. From the beginning the pupils are taught to check their work.

THE PROBLEM MATERIAL 

a. Each new step in a process is presented in some situation in which it is needed and as it functions in life. b. The development of the processes is paralleled closely by the problem work, which applies the processes in life situations. c. The problem content stresses the social values of arithmetic and their applications to the life of the child in and out of school. d. The pupil is frequently given cues to direct him in the solution of problems. e. Definite practice exercises on problem solving are found in the materials for this grade as well as in the other grades. f. At the end of each chapter are problem scales which have been carefully standardized. These scales measure the progress during the year of the pupil in the ability to solve problems. g. At the end of many of the problem units there are suggested activities and starred problems which suggest ways of applying what has been learned to situations both in and out of school. 

The ability of the pupils to apply what has been learned to a real situation is the proof needed that the arithmetic he is learning actually functions. h. The problems are stated clearly and simply in the vocabulary of the children of this grade.

THE SCIENTIFIC DISTRIBUTION OF NUMBER FACTS AND PROCESSES 

a. In the development of the drill exercises on the fundamentals, special attention has been given to the scientific distribution of the number facts according to their learning difficulty. b. Special stress has been placed on the necessity of giving the pupils contact with the varied types of examples in each of the

vi 

INTRODUCTION 

processes. This has been found to be necessary since examples differ greatly among themselves in the specific methods by which they are solved, and the pupils should be given an opportunity to solve them in their drill work under the supervision of the teacher. c. The care with which the steps in the development of the processes in fractions and decimals have been analyzed will be evident from a survey of the pages in which these processes are developed. d. Standardized tests in all processes are found throughout the book. The standards that have been set up are reasonable speed and 100 per cent accuracy.

PROVISION FOR INDIVIDUAL DIFFERENCES 

a. The diagnostic tests make it possible for the teacher to determine the nature of the difficulties of the individual pupils. b. Pupils are directed to do special work on the particular types of examples which they find difficult in the diagnostic tests. c. The practice exercises in each of the processes make it possible for the teacher to determine the particular process in which the pupil is weak, and the element in each process which may be causing difficulty. These practice tests in fractions and decimals begin with simple elements and proceed to others that are increasingly difficult. Each test is therefore a scale by means of which the teacher can determine the level of the achievement of the individual pupils on repeated standardized measurements. d. The problem scales make it possible for the teacher to select the pupils who are weak in problem solving. e. At the end of many of the problem units there are starred problems. These are either more difficult problems than the others in the group, or are suggested activities which the teacher may assign to the superior pupils who have completed the minimum work expected of the class.

CHAPTER I USES OF ARITHMETIC 

1. The Hayes School and the Johnson School had a field meet. John threw the basket ball 44 feet and Peter threw it 46 feet 4 inches. 

How much farther than John did Peter throw the ball? 

Show how arithmetic is used in games that you play. 

2. Tom bought 4 pounds of butter at 45¢ a pound. He gave the clerk a five-dollar bill in payment. 

What change should Tom receive? 

Make a list of ways in which a grocer uses arithmetic. 

One fifth-grade girl listed thirty-one ways. 

3. Name and give the value of all the United States coins that you know. If you had one of each, how much money would you have? Why do we need so many different coins? 

4. Mary is cooking oatmeal. She put the oatmeal into boiling water at 12 minutes to seven. If the oatmeal must boil 20 minutes, at what time will it be cooked? 

5. Make a list of at least six ways in which arithmetic is used in your home. 

6. How does the man at the gasoline station use arithmetic? 

7. Show how you use arithmetic in drawing, geography, history, and music. (1)

JACK'S AUTOMOBILE TRIP 

Last summer Jack went on an automobile trip with his father. Jack was asked to keep a record of the money that was spent on the trip. Here are some of the problems that Jack had to solve. 

1. On the first day Jack's father bought 16 gallons of gasoline at 24 cents a gallon. What did he pay for the gasoline? 

2. On the second day he bought 5 gallons of gasoline. 

He paid the garage man $1.25 for the gasoline. What was the price a gallon? 

3. How much more than on the first day did the gasoline cost a gallon on the second day? 

4. On the third day they bought 13 gallons of gasoline at 26 cents a gallon, and on the fourth day 14 gallons at 25 cents a gallon. What did the gasoline bought on these two days cost? 

5. How many gallons did they buy in all on the four days?

IMPORTANCE OF CHECKING CHECKING YOUR WORK 

5 The best way to see if your work is correct is to check it. Study the following examples carefully. 8835 88358 86 86 59 59 43 43 57 57 96 96 341 341 

TO CHECK ADDITION 

After you have added the numbers by beginning at the top and have written the sum, add by beginning at the bottom. 

If the sums are the same, the work is probably correct. 3,954 *Minuend 

2,386 Subtrahend 

1,568 Difference 3,954 *Check sum 

TO CHECK SUBTRACTION 

After you have found the difference, add the subtrahend and the difference. 

If the sum is the same as the minuend, the work is probably correct. 45 X21 21 X45 45 105 90 84 945* 945* 

TO CHECK MULTIPLICATION 

Interchange the multiplier and multiplicand, and multiply. If the products are the same, the work is probably correct. 

TO CHECK DIVISION 

174 and 4 rem. 5)874* 

The check. 174 X5 870 874* 

Multiply the quotient by the divisor and add to the product the exact remainder, if there is one. If the answer to this work is the same as the dividend, the work is probably correct.

EQUIPPING THE SCHOOL PLAYGROUND 

1. The children in the Hoover School bought a slide for the school playground for $24.75 and a trapeze for $10.75. 

How much did the slide and trapeze cost together? (You will use this answer in problem 7.) 

2. To earn the money to pay for the equipment the school gave a puppet show. The girls sold 327 tickets and the boys sold 298 tickets. How many tickets more than the boys did the girls sell? 

3. How many tickets did the boys and girls sell together? 

4. The tickets were sold for 15 cents each. was received for the tickets sold by the girls? 

How much 

5. How much was received for the tickets sold by the boys? 

6. How much was received for all of the tickets that were sold? (You will use this answer in problem 7.) 

7. How much money more than was needed to pay for the slide and trapeze was received from the sale of tickets? *8. Look up in catalogs the prices of playground equipment which you would like to have on your school playground.

GRADE FIVE 

THE SCHOOL LUNCH 

1. Betty pays 25 cents each day for a school lunch. 

How much does she spend for her lunches in 20 days? 

2. In her school 250 children buy lunches each day. If each child spends on the average 18 cents for lunch, how much is received from the lunches that are sold each day? 

3. Betty's chum, Margaret, kept a record of the amount she spent for lunch each day for a week. She found that she spent $.80 in 5 days. How much did she spend a day on the average? 

4. One noon Betty bought soup for 8 cents, a sandwich for 5 cents, a banana for 2 cents, a glass of milk for 4 cents, and a piece of cake for 3 cents. How much did she spend for lunch? 

5. One noon the children of the school ate 53 loaves of bread and drank 175 quarts of milk. The manager of the lunch room paid 12 cents a loaf for the bread and 11 cents a quart for the milk. How much did he pay in all for the bread and milk which the children ate that noon? 

6. One week the manager bought 19 pounds of butter. 

How much did the butter cost at 49 cents a pound?

13

GRADE FIVE A READING EXERCISE 

I. The fifth- and sixth-grade children were to have a sleighride party. There were 46 fifth-grade pupils and 58 sixthgrade pupils. How many pupils were there in the party if they all went? 

1. Which of the following facts are you asked to find? a. The number of pupils in the fifth grade. b. The number of pupils in the party if they all went. c. The number of pupils who did not go. d. The number of pupils who could ride in one sleigh. 

2. Which of the following facts is given in the problem? a. The number of pupils who could not pay. b. The number of sleighs which would be needed. c. The number of pupils who could not go. d. The number of pupils in the two grades. 

3. In which of the following ways would you work this problem? a. Add 58 and 46. b. Subtract 46 from 58. 

4. Which of the following correct answer? c. Multiply 58 by 46. d. Divide 58 by 46. answers is nearest to the a. 94 pupils. b. 82 pupils. c. 106 pupils. d. 12 pupils. 

II. Only 94 pupils can go on this sleigh ride. If each pupil is given three sandwiches, how many must be ordered for the party? 

1. Which of the following facts are you asked to find? a. The cost of the sandwiches. b. The number of sandwiches for each pupil. c. The total number of sandwiches to be ordered. d. The number of pupils who want sandwiches.

SOLVING PROBLEMS 

15 

2. Which of the following facts is given in the problem? a. The cost of the sandwiches a pupil. b. The number of sandwiches for the fifth grade. c. The number of sandwiches for each pupil and the number of pupils who can go. d. The number of sandwiches not eaten. 

3. In which of the following ways would you work this problem? a. Multiply 94 by 3. b. Divide 94 by 3. c. Subtract 3 from 94. d. Add 3 to 94. 

4. Which of the following is the correct answer? a. 282 sandwiches. c. 272 sandwiches. b. 91 sandwiches. d. 97 sandwiches.

LEARNING TO SOLVE PROBLEMS 

Read each problem carefully. Be sure that you understand all the words. about each problem. 

Ask yourself questions a, b, and c 

Then solve it and check your work. a. What am I asked to find in the problem? b. What things are told in the problem that will help me to find the answer? c. What must I do to solve the problem? 

1. Jack wants a pair of skates. The price of the skates is $4.80. Jack can save $.80 a week. In how many weeks will he save enough money to buy the skates? 

2. Fred took a trip with his father. When they started, the speedometer on the car read 8,569 miles. When they returned home, it read 10,433 miles. How many miles did they travel on their trip? 

3. Jennie spent $8.25 for cloth for a dress. She bought 

3 yards. What did the cloth cost a yard?

GRADE FIVE 

4. A railroad car when empty weighs 33,254 pounds.

When filled with coal, it weighs 86,564 pounds. How much does the coal weigh? 

5. A baseball team bought a bat for $1.60 and a ball for $1.50. What did the bat and ball cost together? 

6. Jennie paid $2.00 for a bag of sugar. If the price of the sugar was 8¢ a pound, how many pounds did she buy? 

7. Out of a list of 50 words in a test, Mary spelled 9 incorrectly. How many words did she spell correctly? 

8. John raises chickens. One week he bought 3 bags of mixed feed at $1.65 a bag. What did he pay for the feed? 

9. Three sacks of oats together weighed 224 pounds.

There were 7 bushels of oats in the three sacks. How many pounds are there in a bushel of oats? 

10. What did May pay for 6 dozen oranges which she bought at 45¢ a dozen? 

11. Julia has $4.25. How much more than this must she have before she can buy a $5 toilet set? 

12. John has been collecting stamps. He has 198 stamps.

They cover 9 pages in his stamp book. How many stamps, on the average, are on each page? 

13. On a reading test Margaret read 567 words in three minutes. How many words did she read, on the average, in one minute? 

14. One Saturday Harry delivered 18 orders for Mr. 

Smith, the grocer. He was paid 5 cents for each order he delivered. How much did Harry receive in all? 

15. One Christmas Janet bought a penknife for $1.50 for her father, a book for $1.75 for her mother, and a toy sailboat for $.75 for her brother. What did she spend in all?

GRADE FIVE HOW TO READ AND WRITE LARGE NUMBERS 

In newspapers, books, and magazines, numbers are often used that are very large. In order that you may be able to read such large numbers easily and accurately, study the following work.

The population of the United States according to the last census was 117,859,495. 

The number is read one hundred seventeen million, eight hundred fifty-nine thousand, four hundred ninety-five.

Large numbers are divided by commas into periods of three figures each, beginning at the right. This table will help you to remember the name of each period.

MILLIONS 

117 

THOUSANDS 

859 

UNITS 

495 In reading a whole number, each period, except the units, is named. $525,000,000 is read five hundred twenty-five million dollars, since there are no numbers in the thousands or units periods.

Copy these numbers, divide them into periods, and be ready to read them: 1. 785926 2. 48653 3. 4. 125789 11206804 5. 859642 6. 458763284

25 In finding answers to problems you must add, subtract, multiply, or divide. Sometimes you must use more than one of these processes. This exercise will give you practice in selecting the process to use.

5. How can you find the total number of children in a school if you know the number of children in each grade? 

6. How can you find your increase in weight during a year? 

7. If you work 8 hours for 25¢ an hour, how can you find what you have earned? 

8. How can you find the number of gallons a 20-quart jar will hold?

8 pounds will cost?

GRADE FIVE COMPARING SHORT AND LONG DIVISION 

When we have easy division examples we write only the answer. When the examples are difficult we write all of the work. When the work is done mentally, and only the answer is written, we use short division. When all the work is written, we use long division. Study the following example worked by short and by long division. 

SHORT DIVISION 

LONG

DIVISION 

1,526 5)7,630 1,526 5)7,630 26 25 13 

You can see that the answer is the same by both methods. 

Notice in the long division example that 5 is written under 7. Instead of carrying the 2, it is written under the 5. 

The 26 is written by bringing down the 6. 

Where do we get 25? 

Where did we get 13? 10 30 

Where did we get 10? 30 

Where did we get 30?

THE STEPS IN LONG DIVISION 

Charley is going with his father on a trip of 672 miles.

Their automobile uses a gallon of gasoline each 21 miles it travels. How many gallons will the car use on the trip?

THE STEPS IN LONG DIVISION 

43 To solve the problem divide 672 by 21. We do this by 

We write out the work instead of doing it long division. mentally. 

I. To find the first quotient figure divide 67 by 21. See 21)67. 21 672 63 42 3. 

Step 1. Test for quotient figure. Think 2)6 

Then 21 is probably contained in 67 three times. Try 3 as your first quotient figure. 

Step 2. Place quotient figure. Write 3 over the 

7 as we are dividing 67 by 21. 

Step 3. Multiply 21 by 3. Write 63 under 67. 

Step 4. Compare 63 with 67 to see that it is not larger than 67. 

Step 5. Subtract 63 from 67. Write 4. 

Step 6. Compare 4 with 21 to see that your remainder is less than your divisor. 

Step 7. Bring down 2, placing it after the 4. 

II. To find the second quotient figure divide 42 by 21. See 

21)42. Begin again with Step 1. 32 21)672 63 42 42 

Step 1. Test for quotient figure. Think 2)4 = 2. 

Then 21 is probably contained in 42 two times. Try 2 as your second quotient figure. 

Step 2. Place quotient figure. the 3. 

Write 2 after 

Step 3. Multiply 21 by 2. Write 42 under 42. 

Step 4. Compare 42 with 42 to see that it is not greater. 

Step 5. Subtract 42 from 42. There is no remainder. Draw a line. 

Check your work: 21 X 32 = 672.

It will require 32 gallons of gasoline 

The quotient is 32. to make the trip of 672 miles. 

To check: 21 X 32 = 672.

GRADE FIVE REMAINDERS IN LONG DIVISION 

Helen gathered 148 eggs. How many dozen could she sell? How many would be left over? 12 dozen 12)148 12 28 24 4 remaining Multiply 

12 by 12 and 12 

To check: X12 24 12 add the remainder 4 144 +4 148 

Some things to remember: a. The remainder is the number left over after the number containing the last figure of the dividend has been divided by the divisor. b. The remainder must be less than the divisor. c. To prove an example with a remainder, multiply the quotient by the divisor, and add the remainder to the product of these two numbers. The sum should equal the dividend.

Here is a short cut in long division.

GRADE FIVE MEASURING LONG DISTANCES 

Yards, feet, and inches are used to measure short lengths or distances. Long distances are measured in other ways. 

1. How long an automobile trip have you taken? 

2. Children in some cities measure the distance from their homes to school by counting the number of blocks they must walk. 

A long block is 660 feet. A short block is 264 feet. 

How many yards is a long block? a short block? 

3. Harry Johnson, who lives in the country, must walk a mile to get to his school. A mile is as long as 8 long blocks. 

How many yards are there in 8 long blocks? How many feet?

GRADE FIVE LEARNING HOW TO SOLVE PROBLEMS 

1. If you know the number of miles an automobile is going an hour, how would you find the number of miles it will travel in 8 hours at this rate? 

2. If you know the price of sugar a pound, how will you find the number of pounds you can buy for a dollar? 

3. If you know the amount a man pays for the rent of his house for one month, how will you find what rent he pays for a year? 

4. If you know how many miles an automobile can travel in one hour, how will you find the number of hours it will take it to travel 2,000 miles? 

5. In a large orchard the trees are in rows with the same number of trees in each row. If you know the number of rows and the number of trees in each row, how will you find the number of trees in the orchard? 

6. If you know the cost of a bushel of wheat, how will you find the cost of 56 bushels of wheat? 

7. If you know how many people there are in New York and how many people there are in Kansas City, how will you find how many more people there are in New York than in Kansas City? 

8. If you know the number of pupils in each room of a large city school, how will you find how many pupils there are in the school? 

9. How do you change feet to yards?

ON A NEW ENGLAND FARM 

1. Scott MacDonald is a farmer's son who lives in northern Maine. One winter the snow fell November 15, and there was snow on the ground until March 15. How many months was there snow on the ground that year? 

2. During that winter Scott made some money by trapping. He caught 25 muskrats, 4 minks, and 3 skunks.

How many animals did Scott catch in his traps? 

3. Scott received, on the average, $1.39 for muskrat skins. What did he receive for his 25 muskrat skins? 

4. He sold his mink skins for $3.60 each. What did he receive for the 4 mink skins?

CHAPTER III A DAIRY FARM 

1. My name is Winifred. I live on a dairy farm. This is a picture of my sister Bell and our two calves. Her calf is White Face. My calf is Spot. and White Face weighs 59 pounds. 

Spot weighs 67 pounds a. How much do they weigh together? b. How much less than Spot does White Face weigh? 

2. My father says that when they are five years old they should weigh together a ton. How many pounds must they gain in 5 years to weigh a ton? 

3. My father has a farm of 276 acres. He says of our land is used for pasture. How many acres of pasture have we on our farm? 

4. We have 31 milk cows. My father weighs the milk we get every day. We got 992 pounds yesterday. How many pounds did this average for each cow? (71)

LONG DIVISION 

You must be very careful in working the following examples, or you may write a quotient which is either too large or too small. Try in every example to write the correct quotient the first time. Here is an example which will help you: 63)4,285 

See 6)42. Think 7. 63)4,285 378 

Multiply 7 × 63 mentally. Say 441. 

Compare 441 with 428. 441 is larger than 428. 

Try 6. 6 X 63 378. 378 is less than 428. 

Therefore the quotient is 6.

How many miles a day did they average?

Write a question that will make this a subtraction problem.

Write a question that will make this a multiplication problem.

Write a question that will make this a division problem.

Write a question that will make this an addition problem.

Write a question that will make this a subtraction problem.

Uncle Ned. He received $13.50 for the work.

Oranges cost 5 cents each.

GRADE FIVE PROBLEM STUDY 

In each of these problems tell, first, what you are asked to find; second, the facts you are told; third, how you will find the answer. Then solve the problem and check your answer. 

1. Jack wants a pair of skates which he saw in a store window. The price is $4.80. Jack can save $.80 a week. 

In how many weeks can he save enough money to buy the skates? 

2. Fred took an auto trip with his father. When they started the speedometer registered 8,569 miles. When they reached home it showed 10,433 miles. 

How many miles did they travel on their trip? 

3. Jennie spent $8.28 for the cloth for a dress. She bought 3 yards. What did the cloth cost a yard? 

4. A railroad car when empty weighs 33,254 pounds.

When filled with coal it weighs 86,564 pounds. How much does the coal weigh? 

5. The Marcy baseball team bought a bat for $1.60 and a ball for $1.50. What did the bat and ball cost? 

6. Jennie paid two dollars for sugar. If the price of the sugar was 8¢ a pound, how many pounds did she buy? 

7. Out of a list of 50 words in a spelling test, Mary missed 9. How many words did she spell correctly? 

8. Three sacks of oats together weighed 192 pounds. 

A bushel of oats weighs 32 pounds. How many bushels of oats were there in the three sacks?

TWO-STEP PROBLEMS HOW TO SOLVE TWO-STEP PROBLEMS 

83 In the following problem you are asked to find the answers to two questions, a and b. The important question is b, but in order to find the answer to b you must first find the answer to a. 

Work this problem: 

1. Albert delivers 5 morning papers and 7 evening papers. a. How many papers does he deliver in 1 day? b. How many papers does he deliver in 6 days?

We can write the problem in a shorter way as you see below.

Albert delivers 5 morning papers and 7 evening papers. 

How many papers does he deliver in 6 days? 

You can see that only the important question b is asked in this problem. But we have learned that we cannot find the answer to b until we first find the answer to question a. We have put question a in hiding without changing the work we must do to solve the problem. 

Rewrite the following problems, putting question a in each problem in hiding as we have done in the boxed explanation of problem 1. Then work each problem. 

2. Mary attends school 3 hours in the morning and 

2 hours in the afternoon. a. How many hours does she spend in school in 1 day? b. How many hours is she in school in 5 days?

GRADE FIVE 

3. Oranges are selling for 60¢ a dozen. a. What is the price of 1 orange? b. What should you pay for 8 oranges?

The following are problems like those you have been making. In each problem find the hidden question. Write it. Name it a. Name the question asked in the problem b. 

After you have done this, solve the problem. The first one is worked for you. Work the others in the same way. 

4. Tom's chickens lay, on the average, 7 eggs a day. 

How many eggs do they lay in 4 weeks? 

The question the problem asks: How many eggs do they lay in 4 weeks? (b) 

What must you first know in order to solve b? The number of eggs the chickens lay in 1 week. 

How many eggs do they lay in 1 week? Call this question a.

SOLUTION 

7 eggs in 1 day

X7 

49 eggs in 1 week

X4 

196 eggs in 4 weeks 

49 eggs in 1 week 

5. Mary and Agnes made lemonade and sold it one hot day. They paid 15¢ for the lemons and 12¢ for sugar. 

They received 75¢ for the lemonade. How much money did they make on their lemonade sale? 

6. The fare on the street car to the city is 8¢. Children pay half fare. What is the fare for 7 children?

PROBLEMS WITH TWO QUESTIONS SOLVING PROBLEMS WITH TWO QUESTIONS 

85 

1. Mary bought an apple for 4 cents and a candy bar for 5 cents. She gave the clerk a quarter. a. What did the apple and the candy bar cost her? b. What change did the clerk give her? 

2. Henry picked 5 bouquets of flowers. 

He gave 2 of them to his mother and sold the others for 15 cents each. a. How many bouquets did he sell? b. What did he receive for those he sold? 

3. Alice had 15 cents. She bought 2 ice-cream cones for 5 cents apiece. a. What did she spend for the cones? b. How much money did she have left? 

4. John bought a pound of butter for 46 cents and 2 loaves of bread at 14 cents a loaf. a. What did the bread cost? b. What did he spend for the bread and the butter? 

5. Harry bought 2 apples for 8 cents and a candy bar for 5 cents. a. What did Harry pay for one apple? b. How much more than the cost of one apple was the cost of the candy bar? 

6. Alice bought 3 yards of blue ribbon and 4 yards of white ribbon. The ribbon cost 25 cents a yard. a. How many yards of ribbon did Alice buy? b. What did she pay for the ribbon?

GRADE FIVE 

7. Jack sells newspapers at 2 cents each. One day he 

He buys 5-cent pencils with the money sold 10 papers. he receives from the papers. a. What did he receive from the sale of 10 papers? b. How many 5-cent pencils can he buy with this money? 

8. Alice had 15 cents. She spent 10 cents for a tablet. 

That night her father gave her 25 cents. a. How much did Alice have left after she bought the tablet? b. How much did she have after her father gave her the 

25 cents? 

9. Henry had 5 rows of tomato plants in his garden with 4 plants in each row. The plants cost him 2 cents apiece. a. How many plants did he have in all? b. What did the plants cost at 2 cents apiece? 

10. In a fifth-grade class there are 12 boys and 16 girls.

They are to march by fours into the auditorium. a. How many children in all are there in the room? b. How many groups of four are there? 

11. Tom spent 10¢ for a drawing pencil and 15¢ for a notebook. He gave the clerk a dollar bill. a. How much did he spend for both the pencil and the notebook? b. What change should he receive? 

12. Rewrite each of these problems. Put question a in hiding in each problem.

In subtracting 64 from 66, the remainder is 2. 

Bringing down the 2 from 6,624 makes the trial dividend 22. 32 is not contained in 22. So we write 0 in the quotient to show that 32 was not contained in 22. 

Bring down the 4, making the number 224. 

Divide 224 by 32.

HOW PEOPLE TRAVEL HOW PEOPLE TRAVEL 

95 

1. Jack rode on horseback from his home to that of his uncle, a distance of 36 miles. It took him 6 hours to make the trip. How many minutes did it take him to ride one mile? 

2. Jim, his brother, has a bicycle. It takes him 9 minutes to travel a mile. How many minutes will it take him to go from his home to his uncle's? 

3. An Eskimo started out on a long journey of 406 miles with his dog team. Because the snow was very deep, he traveled only 29 miles a day. At this rate how many days did it take him to make the trip? 

4. Tom's parents are planning a trip to their summer home 189 miles away. They can travel an average of 27 miles an hour in their car. How many hours will it take them to drive to their summer home? 

5. A fast train takes 22 hours to go from Chicago to 

New York, a distance of 1,012 miles. How many miles does it travel in an hour? 

6. An airplane flies at the rate of 72 miles an hour. How many miles can it travel in 17 hours? 

7. In 1926 Commander Byrd flew over the North Pole.

He flew from Spitzbergen and back again, a distance of 

1,584 miles, in 16 hours. How many miles did he travel, on the average, in an hour? *8. A steamship left New York at noon on Monday.

If it travels at the rate of 22 miles an hour, how far from 

New York will it be on Wednesday afternoon at five o'clock?

HOW PRODUCTS ARE SHIPPED HOW PRODUCTS ARE SHIPPED 

99 When we ship anything from one place to another we pay the company that carries the shipment for us. Small parcels or amounts are shipped by express, and we pay express charges. Heavy objects or large quantities are shipped by freight, and we pay freight charges. 

Name some things that are shipped by freight; by express. 

Dairy Problems 

1. Mr. Smith shipped butter to town by express. He sold it for $23.00. The cost of shipping was $2.74. How much of this money did he have after he paid the shipping charges? 

2. At another time he shipped butter which he sold for $35.00. How much of this money did he have left after he had paid the shipping charges which were $3.98? 

3. Mr. Smith sold 25 gallons of milk at 28¢ a gallon. 

How much did he receive for his milk? 

4. The grocer gave him a check for $36.00 for eggs.

The cost of the crates for the eggs was $3.20. How much of this money did he have left after paying for the crates? 

5. Mr. Smith hauls his dairy products to the station by auto in 15 minutes. The train that takes his milk to the city is due at 6:15 A.M. At what time must Mr. Smith leave home to reach the station 15 minutes before the train is due?

CHAPTER IV THE COTTON BELT 

My name is Helen. I am a fifth-grade girl living in the Cotton Belt. I shall give you some problems about cotton. 

1. My father has a plantation of 356 acres, all under cultivation. This year one fourth of it was planted to cotton. How many acres are planted to cotton? 

2. How many acres of our plantation are planted to other things? 

3. We planted the cotton on April 18. On April 30 we could see the first little sprouts pushing their way through the ground. How many days after the cotton was planted did we see the first sprouts appear? 

4. In planting the fields a cotton planter is used. It plants 8 acres a day. How many days does it take our planter to plant 88 acres of cotton? (107)

GRADE FIVE 

5. After the cotton has sprouted, we spent many hours in "chopping," that is, thinning out the plants. The plants are left 8 inches apart. How many plants would there be in a row 318 feet long? 

Think first: How many inches are there in a row?

As there will be a plant at each end of a row, there will be one more plant than you will find in solving this problem. Show this by a diagram for the following problem:

If we set the plants one inch apart, how many plants would there be in a row 3 inches long? 

6. In the middle of August some of the cotton was ready to pick. On September 1 we had nine pickers in the fields. 

My father weighed the cotton each picked, and kept an account of it. This is the number of pounds each picked that day: 

Nellie 155 lb. 

Ned 130 lb. 

Linda 120 lb. 

Jim 125 lb. 

Susie 140 lb. 

Tom 115 lb. 

Delia 140 lb. 

Sam 120 lb. 

George 110 lb.

How many pounds of cotton did all the pickers pick? 

7. When cotton is picked it contains so many seeds that it is called "seed cotton." Seeds make up about of the weight of the seed cotton. How many pounds of seeds are there in the "seed cotton" picked by each of the pickers on September 1? 

8. After the cotton was picked it was taken to the gin to have the seeds taken out. After this, it was put up into bales weighing 500 pounds each. How many pounds of cotton are there in 31 bales?

PRACTICE WITH LONG DIVISION 

109 9. The part of cotton used for making cloth is lint.

The rest of the seed cotton is made up of fuzz around the seeds, and is called "linters." How many pounds of linters are there? 

11. The boll weevil is the worst enemy of the cotton plant. It was first seen in our country in 1892. How long ago was that? *12. Cotton should have 200 days without frost in the growing season to ripen. Where we live there is very seldom any frost after April 1 and very seldom any before 

November 20. Is this season long enough to raise cotton successfully?

GRADE FIVE SOLVING TWO-STEP PROBLEMS 

1. The children in grades 3 and 4 used 25 half-pint bottles of milk each morning. The children in grades 1 and 2 used 

43 half-pint bottles. What was the cost of the milk at 3¢ a bottle? a. What am I to find? 

The cost of the milk. b. What facts are given? 

Grades 3 and 4 used 25 half-pint bottles. 

Grades 1 and 2 used 43 half-pint bottles. 

The cost a bottle is 3¢. c. How can I find the answer?

The number of bottles used times the price of one bottle equals the cost of the milk. 

How many bottles were used? The fact is not given in the problem and must be found.

First Step 

25 bottles in grades 3 and 4 

43 bottles in grades 1 and 2 

68 bottles used d. Is the answer reasonable?

Second Step 

Finding the answer: 

68 X $.03 = $2.04

Answer questions a, b, c, and d about the two problems which follow: 

2. The children in a school wish to raise money to buy some swings for the playground at a cost of $85. They sold 258 tickets for a school fair at 10¢ each. How much must they still earn before they can pay for the swings? 

3. One day Alice picked 15 bunches of flowers. She gave three of the bunches to her mother and sold the rest at 15 cents a bunch. What did she receive for the flowers she sold?

EVERYDAY ARITHMETIC EVERYDAY ARITHMETIC 

111 

1. Ten boys gave 15¢ each and sent Don and Fred to buy a baseball. If the ball cost $1.35, how much money did they bring back? 

2. In the morning David had 19 marbles. He lost 11 of them on the way to school. That afternoon his brother gave him 25 marbles. How many marbles did he then have? 

3. A party of 14 boys and 11 girls hired a man to row them across a river. How many trips must the boatman make if he can take only five passengers across at one time? 

4. Mr. Brown was driving his car home from a lake 12 miles from town. He had gone only 3 miles when the car broke down. The garage man charged him 50¢ a mile for towing him into town. How much did Mr. Brown pay to the garage man? 

5. Eight members of a girls' canning club planned to can three kinds of fruit during a certain week in order to help fill a community barrel. Each girl was to can two jars of each kind of fruit. How many jars of fruit did the girls prepare for the barrel? 

6. A party cost 60¢ and there were six children to pay for it. If Ruth's father gave her 25¢, how much did she have left after she paid her share of the cost of the party? 

7. Frank had fifteen rabbits. Harold had six more than 

Frank. How many rabbits did the two boys have? 

8. Frank sells 10 papers a day and makes 2¢ on each paper. How long will it take him to earn enough to buy a jackknife which costs 80¢? 

9. Some girls were planning a 

They were to leave at 8 o'clock. hiking trip of 8 miles. 

If they walked, on the

GRADE FIVE 

average, about two miles an hour, at what time would they reach home? 

10. Pearl had 35¢. Louise had 10¢ less than Pearl. If 

Louise spent 15¢ for candy, how many cents would she have left? 

11. A farmer paid 5¢ a basket to two boys for picking up potatoes. Robert picked up 21 baskets and Fred picked up 17 baskets. How much money did the two boys earn? 

12. William's father gave him a ball of kite string 600 feet long. William divided the string into three equal pieces. How many yards long was each piece? 

13. Twenty-one Boy Scouts from one town and seventeen from another were camping at a lake. If only thirty-two could sleep in the cottages, how many slept in the tents? 

14. Mary invited eighteen of her friends to a picnic.

Seven could not come. In planning the boat rides, she was told by the man at the boathouse that she could have four people in each boat. How many boats did she need? 

15. A class of 36 pupils was divided into four equal groups for a contest. 

If each one in Harry's group won 3 points the first day of the contest, find the number of points his group won. 

16. Three brothers wished to visit their cousin on the other side of the town. If street-car fare cost 20¢ for each boy and they wanted 30¢ in all for spending money, how much money should they take with them? 

17. The fifth-grade class in the Packer School wishes to buy 2 pictures that cost $3.50 each. There is only $5.20 in the class fund. How much more money must be earned before the class can buy the pictures?

How much did he receive for all the papers that he sold?

VOYAGES OF COLUMBUS 

121 The Santa Maria, flagship of Columbus, beside the Leviathan.

USING ARITHMETIC IN HISTORY 

On his first voyage to America Columbus set sail from Spain August 3, 1492. He was delayed for some time at the Canary Islands, but finally sailed from these islands on 

September 6, 1492. He landed the following October 12 on one of the islands which we now call the West Indies. 

1. How long did it take Columbus to sail from the Canary Islands to the West Indies? How many days does it take one of our large ocean liners to cross the Atlantic Ocean? 

How much faster than the voyage of Columbus is this? 

2. The distance from the Canary Islands to the West 

Indies is about 3,500 miles. How many miles did Columbus sail a day on the average, allowing 35 full days for the trip? 

3. The Leviathan, one of our largest ocean liners, travels about 25 miles an hour. In how many hours can it travel 

3,500 miles? How many hours more than 5 days is this? 

4. The largest vessel in the fleet of Columbus was the 

Santa Maria. This ship was 128 feet long, 25 feet wide, and

GRADE FIVE 

15 feet deep. The Leviathan is 907 feet long, 100 feet wide, and 58 feet deep. How much longer than the Santa 

Maria is the Leviathan? how much wider? how much deeper? 

5. A city block is about 600 feet long. How much longer than such a block is the Leviathan? Measure off a distance on some street as long as the length of the Leviathan. 

Beginning at the same point, measure off the length of the Santa Maria. This will give you an idea of the size of the ships in which Columbus made his voyage. 

6. Columbus kept a journal of the distance his ships sailed each day. This is his record for the first week in 

October. He measured the distance in leagues. miles make one Italian league. Four 

October 1.... 

25 leagues 

October 4. 

63 leagues

October 2. 

41 leagues 

October 5. 

57 leagues 

October 3. 

47 leagues 

October 6. 

40 leagues 

October 7...... 

27 leagues 

How many leagues did the fleet sail in the seven days? 

How many miles was this? 

7. In how many hours can the Leviathan travel as far as the fleet of Columbus sailed in these seven days? 

8. Account for the fact that the fleet of Columbus did not sail as far on some days as on others. Do our modern steamships usually do this? Why? 

9. On the three vessels in the fleet of Columbus there were 90 seamen and 30 officers. How many men a ship did this average? 

10. On a trip across the Atlantic during the World War, the Leviathan carried 3,500 soldiers. How many more than the average number of persons on each ship in Columbus's fleet was this?

OWNING A BICYCLE THE COST OF OWNING A BICYCLE 

131 

1. Elizabeth and John belong to the Journey Bicycle 

Club. Elizabeth's bicycle cost her $18.75. She borrowed this money from her father, who is to deduct 75 cents a month from her allowance. How many months will it take Elizabeth to pay for the bicycle? 

2. John's bicycle cost him $22.50. He had saved $9.75, which he paid in cash to Mr. Scroat, the bicycle dealer.

John agreed to pay the remainder in equal amounts each week for 10 weeks. How much did he pay each week under this plan? 

3. John holds the club speed record for distance riding. 

One week he rode the 15 miles to the club camp in 2 hours. 

How many miles an hour did he average? 

4. The cyclometer on Elizabeth's bicycle shows that she has ridden 890 miles. John has ridden 1,012 miles on his bicycle. How many miles farther than Elizabeth has 

John ridden? 

5. One spring John bought 2 new tires for $2.35 each and a new inner tube for $.78. How much did he spend for the tires and the tube? 

6. Elizabeth bought a tire for $2.73, a new saddle for $1.58, and a new cyclometer for $.96. How much did she spend for this new equipment? 

7. In three years John spent $14.40 for new tires, other equipment, and repairs. How much did he spend a month on the average for these expenses? 

8. In this time Elizabeth spent $15.12. How much more than John did she spend a month? 

9. How much did John and Elizabeth together spend for repairs?

GRADE FIVE HOUSEHOLD EXPENSES 

Mrs. Johnson always kept a careful record of the money that she spent for rent, clothing, and other household expenses. The following problems tell about some of the expenses during the year. 

1. Mrs. Johnson paid $45 a month for rent. What was the total amount spent for rent during the year? 

2. At the beginning of May she received an electric-light bill for $3.56, a gas bill for $2.98, and a telephone bill for $3.50. What was the total amount of these bills? 

3. At this rate what would be the total amount spent for electricity, gas, and telephone in one year? 

4. The total amount spent for groceries in one year was $496.80. What was the average a month? 

5. Mr. Johnson's salary was $3,600 a year. How much did he receive a month? 

6. Mrs. Johnson subscribed for three magazines. One magazine cost $4.00, another cost $3.50, and the third cost $1.75. Find the cost of the three magazines. 

7. Mr. Johnson bought 11 tons of coal. The coal cost $94.60. What was the average price a ton? 

8. Mrs. Johnson bought 8 baskets of potatoes for $14.00. 

How much did she pay a basket?

PROBLEMS ABOUT COLD STORAGE

PRESERVING FOOD IN COLD STORAGE 

In large cities great quantities of food are preserved in cold-storage plants. Foods of different kinds are stored in rooms in which the temperature is kept always the same. 

However, different temperatures are necessary for different foods. How do we measure temperature? 

1. In a large storage plant there is one floor called the egg floor.

In this room there were 30,000 cases of eggs. 

The temperature in the room is kept always at 29 degrees. 

How much below freezing is this? Use a thermometer to help you to find the answer. 

2. One hot day in August the temperature out of doors was 102 degrees. How much lower than this was the temperature on the egg floor? Remember that the temperature on the egg floor is kept always at 29 degrees. 

3. In each of the cases of eggs on the egg floor there were 30 dozen eggs. How many eggs were there in all?

C-10

GRADE FIVE 

4. On the average each person eats about two eggs a week. How many persons could be supplied for a week with the eggs on the egg floor? 

5. What is the value of the eggs at 39 cents a dozen? 

What would be the value of the eggs at local prices? Cold storage eggs usually do not cost as much as fresh eggs. 

Why is this? 

6. In the same storage house there is a room in which apples are stored. In this room the temperature is kept at 34 degrees. How many degrees warmer than the egg room is the temperature of the apple room? 

7. Is the temperature in the apple room above or below freezing? how much? What would happen to the apples if the temperature fell to 15 degrees above zero? 

8. In another room butter and dressed chickens are stored. The temperature here is always kept at 5 degrees below zero. 

How much lower than freezing is this? 

9. How much colder than in the apple room is the temperature in the butter room? 

10. How much warmer than the butter room is the egg floor? 

11. The next room in the cold-storage plant is piled high with boxes of cheese. The temperature of this room is 28°. How much below freezing is this? 

12. There are 1,624 pounds of cheese in the room. What is the value of this cheese at local prices? 

Show the zero

GRADE FIVE EVERYDAY ARITHMETIC 

Find the answers to as many of these problems as you can in 20 minutes. They are all two-step problems. 

1. Jack had 8 rabbits, and Harry had 6 more than Jack. 

How many rabbits did the two boys have? 

2. George bought a pound of sugar for 7¢, and a package of salt for 10¢. What change should he receive from a quarter? 

3. Mary bought 6 red ribbons and 5 blue ribbons. Find the total cost if each ribbon was priced at 5¢. 

4. John picked 7 quarts of berries on Monday and 

5 quarts on Tuesday. He received 60¢ for his work. What did he receive a quart? 

5. A grocer had two boxes of apples, each containing 

60 pounds. From one of them he sold 30 pounds. How many pounds did he have left? 

6. Harry picked 7 bouquets of sweet peas. 

He gave three bouquets to his mother and sold the rest for 10¢ each. What did he receive for his flowers? 

7. A farmer went to market with 40 baskets of potatoes. 

He sold 30 baskets at $2 each and the remainder he sold for $18. What did he receive a basket for the remainder? 

8. John had $10 in his savings account. He worked 

4 hours on Saturday at $.25 an hour. He put his wages in the savings bank. What did he then have in the bank? 

9. George bought three loaves of bread for 9¢ a loaf. 

Find the change he received from a half dollar. 

10. An automobilist bought 5 gallons of gasoline at 20¢ a gallon. Find the cost a mile for gasoline if he drove 100 miles with this supply.

Three halves are written 3. = ? 

Numbers like 1, 7, 25, and 96 are called whole numbers, or integers. 

Numbers like 11, 22, and 563 are called mixed numbers. 

Fractions like ½, 1, or 7, are called proper fractions. Their numerators are smaller than their denominators. 

Fractions like 2, 4, or 16 are called improper fractions.

Their numerators are equal to or larger than their denominators.

FINDING PERIMETERS

The distance around a surface is called its perimeter. mi. mi.

AREA AND DIMENSIONS MEANING OF AREA AND DIMENSIONS 

A B 203 

1. Which of these two rectangles takes up more space?

The space inclosed by a perimeter is called area. 

We say that rectangle B has a greater area than rectangle A because B takes up more space. 

The area of a farm means the space or surface it covers. 

A large farm has a greater area than a small farm. 

2. Measure the length of rectangle A; of rectangle B. 

3. Which has the greater width?

The length and the width of a rectangle are called its dimensions.

Mary's? James'?

GRADE FIVE SOLVING PROBLEMS BY USE OF DIAGRAMS 

In solving a problem a diagram is often helpful. If you cannot see the diagram in your mind, draw one on paper. 

1. In an orchard there are 4 rows of trees and 7 trees in 

Make a diagram showing how many trees there each row. are in the orchard. 

2. Jack has a stamp book. 

On each page he has 24 stamps arranged in rows. Draw a page showing how Jack might have arranged his stamps. How many rows of stamps might he have on a page? how many stamps in each row? 

3. Mrs. Smith cut a pie into 4 equal parts. picture of the pie. Show how it was cut. Draw a 

4. Draw a pie divided into 8 equal pieces. What part of the pie is each of the 8 equal pieces? Color three of the eight parts. What part of the pie is not colored? 

5. To go to his school, Harry walks east 2 blocks and north 

4 blocks. Make a diagram of the route Harry takes to school. 

6. Draw a picture of a thermometer. Mark on it zero, the freezing point of water, and the temperature at which your schoolroom should be kept. 

7. Draw a face of a clock showing the hours with Roman numerals. Make the hands of the clock show 15 minutes past 5. 

8. Draw a circle. Divide it into 4 equal parts. Color 

2 of it red. 

9. Draw a rectangle whose dimensions are 2 inches by 11 inches. Find the perimeter of the rectangle.

GRADE FIVE 

A SUMMER CAMP 

Each summer the Smith boys go to a summer camp on one of the lakes. 

1. On the way to the camp they go through Standish, which is 12 miles from home. Standish is 5½ miles from the camp. How far is the camp from their home? 

2. The camp is mile from a mail box on the road. In going for the mail each morning and returning to camp, how far does Harry Smith have to walk? 

3. One morning John Smith caught two bass. One of them weighed 12 pounds and the other weighed 13 pounds. 

What did the two fish weigh together? 

4. The law requires that all perch less than 61 inches in length be thrown back into the lake. John caught one that was 67 inches long. How much over the legal limit was this? 

5. John went to the grocery store to get 3 pounds of butter. Butter sold at 36¢ a pound. What did he pay for the butter?

8. It is 23 miles across the lake to the village of Dayton. 

The boat is halfway across. How much farther has it to go? 

9. Mr. Johnson, director of the camp, estimated that the boys used 24 pounds of butter a day. How much would the butter for a week cost at $.40 a pound?
